), we have

\displaystyle\forall_{[w]\in\rho_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{-1}(\{\lambda\})}\ :\ \Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}|_{T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}}=0\,,\ i.e.,\qquad\qquad\Gamma({\mathsf{T}}T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]})\subset{\rm ker}\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,.

Proof.

Cf.
Appendix
E
. ∎

The proof of the observation that vector fields on
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
tangent to the submanifold
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
annihilate
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
is completely straightforward. By contrast, it is far from obvious that the said vector fields span the
whole
kernel of the 2-form over the larger spaces
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
(composed of full
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-orbits), so that the

\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
emerge as candidates for elementary inter-bi-brane worldvolumes. The choice of the smooth space
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
as the point of departure of our search for these elementary worldvolumes here is dictated by the worldsheet interpretation thereof as the connected components of the codomain of the
\sigma
-model field restricted to a defect junction
\,x_{n+1}\,
with
n
incoming defect lines with
field discontinuities from the respective conjugacy classes
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{i}}\,
and a single outgoing one with the limiting value of the field jump given by the product
\,{\rm pr}_{2}\circ\pi_{1,n+1}\circ X(x_{n+1})=({\rm pr}_{2}\circ\pi_{1,2})\circ X(x_{n+1})\cdot({\rm pr}_{2}\circ\pi_{2,3})\circ X(x_{n+1})\cdot\cdots\cdot({\rm pr}_{2}\circ\pi_{n,n+1})\circ X(x_{n+1})
,
cf.
Eq. (
). Arguments of symmetry alone (other than ‘reducibility’) leave unanswered the natural question as to whether connected components of the space
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
can be regarded as elementary inter-bi-brane worldvolumes or they split further into a disjoint union of the manifolds
\,{\rm G}\times T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
with
\,\rho_{\vec{\lambda}}([\overrightarrow{w}])=\lambda
. As complete
{\rm G}_{\rm s}
-orbits, the latter are the
smallest
manifolds on which the 2-isomorphisms
\,\varphi_{n+1}\,
could exist in principle, consistently with the symmetries present. The leaves
\,\mathcal{L}\,
of the characteristic foliation of
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
within
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
(some of them), on the other hand, define – as we shall explain in detail in Sec.
5.4
– the
largest
possible connected components
\,{\rm G}\times\mathcal{L}\,
of the support of the
\,\varphi_{n+1}
. It is one of the main results of the present paper that the leaves
\,\mathcal{L}\,
coincide with the minimal manifolds
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}
. Its proof hinges on a reinterpretation of the 2-form
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
of Eq. (
4.31
) in what may, on the face of it, seem as a completely unrelated mathematical context
24
24

24

We remark, in passing, that the existence of the 2-form
\,\Omega_{2}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
on the submanifold
\,{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{1}})\cap{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{2}})\cap{\rm m}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\lambda})\subset{\rm G}^{2}
, the latter arising in a natural manner in the context of the
anticipated
‘bi-brane fusion’, was observed, as a highly non-trivial ‘fact’, already in Ref.
[
FSW08
]
, with explicit reference to the geometric quantisation of the moduli space of flat connections on a triply punctured Riemann sphere in which this 2-form had been known to play an important rôle.
, to wit, the symplectic geometry of the space of connections on a trivial principal
{\rm G}
-bundle over the Riemann sphere
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}\,
punctured at
n+1
distinct points, admitting a flat connection with fixed holonomies around the punctures. The advantage of rephrasing the problem in hand in this new context is that it enables us to identify the kernel of
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
as a simple corollary to the beautiful result of Alekseev and Malkin reported in Ref.
[
AM95
]
from which – remarkably – the 2-form
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
emerges as the pre-symplectic form on a finite-dimensional space obtained through partial symplectic reduction of the phase space of the Chern–Simons theory with the gauge group
\,{\rm G}\,
on
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}
, coupled to timelike Wilson lines of fixed holonomy. Thus, solving the DJI will require additional physical and mathematical structures of an increasing complexity, which we introduce gradually in the subsequent sections. Our analysis starts, out of necessity, with a brief reminder on the Chern–Simons topological gauge field theory in the presence of timelike Wilson lines,
cf.
Ref.
[
Wit89b
]
, and its relation to (the chiral component of) the WZW
\sigma
-model,
cf.
Refs.
[
Gaw99
,
GTTNB04
,
Gaw02
]
.

4.5.
Insights from the Chern–Simons theory

Our hitherto discussion emphasises the essential rôle of the target geometry for the
boundary
WZW defect and its symmetries as the carrier of the nontrivial topological and cohomological data of the non-boundary WZW defect, fixing, in particular, the labelling of the connected components of the corresponding worldvolume by weights of
\,\mathfrak{g}\,
and determining the associated summands
\,\omega_{\partial,\lambda}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
of the degree-2 components
\,\omega_{\lambda}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
of the simplicial curvature
\,\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}=({\rm H}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\rm C},\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})},0,0)
. This constatation shall be strengthened in Sec.
5
by our study of the geometrisation of the latter simplicial 3-cocycle, and – in particular – extended to the defect intersections, from which the non-boundary completion of the boundary structure emerges as a mere ‘spectator’. All this suggests that we pause to take a closer look at the boundary WZW
\sigma
-model in search of hints as to the geometric meaning of the constraints (
1.26
) that the worldsheet analysis of Ref.
[
RS09
]
imposes upon the worldvolume of the maximally symmetric inter-bi-brane.

The boundary WZW
\sigma
-model was studied at great length in Ref.
[
GTTNB04
]
(
cf.
also Ref.
[
Gaw02
]
), where its (pre)symplectic structure
\,\Omega_{\partial{\rm WZW}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
was demonstrated to decompose, in an adapted vertex-IRF-type parametrisation of the chiral components of the extremal configurations, into the ‘dynamical’ part
\,\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{{\mathsf{L}}{\rm G}}\,
on the so-called model space
\,{\mathsf{L}}{\rm G}\times\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g})\,
of the loop group
\,{\mathsf{L}}{\rm G}
, and the ’boundary’ part
\,\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}\,
depending on the boundary data of the configuration. In the simplest case of a pair:
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0\}\,
and
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times\{\pi\}\,
of connected components of the boundary of a strip worldsheet
\,\Sigma={\mathbb{R}}\times[0,\pi]\,
that are presupposed to be mapped to the respective conjugacy classes
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{0}}\ni g(\sigma^{1},0)\,
and
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{\pi}}\ni g(\sigma^{1},\pi)\,
by an extremal configuration
\,g\in[\Sigma,{\rm G}]
, the latter takes the form
\,g(\sigma)=g_{\rm L}(\sigma^{+})\cdot h_{0}\cdot g_{\rm L}(-\sigma^{-})^{-1}\equiv g_{\rm L}(\sigma^{+}-2\pi)\cdot h_{\pi}\cdot g_{\rm L}(-\sigma^{-})^{-1}
, expressed in terms of an arbitrary twisted loop
\,g_{\rm L}\in[{\mathbb{R}},{\rm G}]\,
with the monodromy
\,\gamma=h_{\pi}\cdot h_{0}^{-1}\equiv{\rm Ad}_{g_{0}}({\mathsf{e}}_{\mu})
, written for
\,(g_{0},\mu)\in{\rm G}\times{\mathsf{k}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g})
, in a conjugacy class with the weight label
\,\mu\,
fixed by the
fusion
of the boundary data
\,(h_{\pi},h_{0})\in{\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{\pi}})\cap{\rm pr}_{2}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{0}})\cap\widetilde{\rm m}{}^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{\mu})\subset{\rm G}^{\times 2}\,
under the binary operation
\,\widetilde{\rm m}\equiv{\rm m}\circ({\rm id}_{\rm G}\times{\rm Inv})\,
whose twist in the second argument accounts for the relative orientation flip of the two components of
\,\partial\Sigma
. In this manner, the boundary data control the topology of the chiral component of the bulk field in the said parametrisation
\,g_{\rm L}(\sigma^{+})=h(\sigma^{+})\cdot{\mathsf{e}}^{{\mathsf{i}}\,\sigma^{+}\frac{\mu}{{\mathsf{k}}}}\cdot g_{0}^{-1}\,
with
\,h\in{\mathsf{L}}{\rm G}
. In this parametrisation, the ‘boundary’ part reads

\displaystyle\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}(h_{\pi},h_{0},g_{0},\mu)=\tfrac{{\mathsf{k}}}{4\pi}\,\widehat{\rm tr}{}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigl(\theta_{\rm L}(h_{0})\wedge\theta_{\rm L}(h_{\pi})\bigr)+{\mathsf{i}}\,\widehat{\rm tr}{}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigl({\mathsf{d}}\mu\wedge\theta_{\rm L}(g_{0})\bigr)+\omega_{\partial,\lambda_{0}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}(h_{0})-\omega_{\partial,\lambda_{\pi}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}(h_{\pi})+\omega_{\partial,\mu}^{({\mathsf{k}})}(\gamma)\,,

where
\,\widehat{\rm tr}{}_{\mathfrak{g}}={\rm id}_{\Omega^{\bullet}({\rm G}^{\times 3}\times{\mathsf{k}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g}))}\otimes{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

One of the key insights of Ref.
[
GTTNB04
]
(inspired by the earlier works
[
Wit89b
,
Gaw89
,
Wit92
,
Gaw99
]
concerned with the (chiral)
bulk
WZW
\sigma
-model and elaborated in Ref.
[
Gaw02
]
) is the identification of the ‘boundary’/topological term
\,\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}\,
in
\,\Omega_{\partial{\rm WZW}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
as a descendant of the presymplectic form of the three-dimensional Chern–Simons (CS) theory with the structure group
\,{\rm G}\,
(as defined in Refs.
[
Sch78
,
Zuc87
,
Wit89b
]
) over the cylinder
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}_{(3)}\,
with the equitemporal slice given by the Riemann sphere
\,{\mathbb{S}}^{2}_{(3)}\cong{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}\setminus\{P_{\lambda_{\pi}},P_{\lambda_{0}},P_{\mu}\}\,
punctured at the three distinct points. At these points, a triple of timelike Wilson lines
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times\{P_{x}\},\ x\in\{\lambda_{\pi},\lambda_{0},\mu\}\,
pierce the sphere. The gauge field of the CS theory is coupled covariantly to these field-strength singularities, and so it acquires a nontrivial holonomy around the singularity lines, further constrained to take values in the respective conjugacy classes
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{\pi}},\ \mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{0}}\,
and
\,\mathscr{C}_{\mu}
. The relevant descent is that to the ‘almost-physical’ phase space of the CS theory with all of the gauge-symmetry group but its finite subgroup
\,{\rm G}\,
divided out in a Marsden–Weinstein-type reduction. The latter was worked out by Alekseev and Malkin in
[
AM95
]
for cylinders
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times\Sigma_{g,n+1}\,
over Riemann surfaces of an arbitrary genus
\,g\,
and an arbitrary number
\,n+1\,
of punctures. We shall now recapitulate it briefly for
\,g=0\,
with view to extracting from it a useful interpretation of the 2-form on the LHS of the DJI for the WZW
\sigma
-model with a maximally symmetric defect. Thus, we shall be concerned with the CS theory on the cylinder
\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}={\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
over the punctured Riemann sphere
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}={\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}\setminus\{P_{i}\}_{i\in\overline{1,n+1}}\,
in the presence of
\,n\,
timelike Wilson lines
\,{\rm W}_{\lambda_{i}},\ i\in\overline{1,n}\,
and a single
anti
-timelike one
\,{\rm W}_{\lambda_{n+1}}\equiv{\rm W}_{\mu}
, localised at the respective singularity lines
\,\ell_{i}={\mathbb{R}}\times\{P_{i}\}\,
of the field-strength 2-form (with the
\,\ell_{i\neq n+1}\,
carrying the standard orientation, and
\,\ell_{n+1}\,
– the opposite one) and having the property that the holonomy of the connection around
\,\ell_{i}\,
takes values in the conjugacy class
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{i}}\subset{\rm G}
.

Let us first review elementary facts about the relevant three-dimensional topological field theory. The basic field variables of the theory are the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection 1-forms
\,\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{A}\otimes t_{A}\,
on (the base of) a trivial principal
{\rm G}
-bundle over
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}\,
which are smooth everywhere except at the excluded lines
\,\ell_{i}\,
(where the corresponding curvature is understood to develop singularities), and the
{\rm G}
-valued fields
\,\gamma_{i}\in[\ell_{i},{\rm G}]\,
supported on the respective singularity lines
\,\ell_{i}
. The action functional takes the form

(4.32)

\displaystyle S_{\rm CS|W}[\mathcal{A},\{\gamma_{i}\}]=-\tfrac{{\mathsf{k}}}{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}\wedge{\mathsf{d}}\mathcal{A}+\tfrac{2}{3}\,\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\bigr)+{\mathsf{k}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\,\int_{\ell_{i}}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigl[\lambda_{i}\,\bigl({\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\gamma_{i}}\circ\mathcal{A}+\gamma_{i}^{*}\theta_{\rm L}\bigr)\bigr]

(4.33)

and is readily verified to be invariant under gauge transformations
\,(\mathcal{A},\gamma_{i})\longmapsto({\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\chi}\circ\mathcal{A}-\chi^{*}\theta_{\rm R},\iota_{i}^{*}\chi\cdot\gamma_{i})\,
engendered by maps
25
25

25

Gauge transformations form a Lie–Fréchet group
\,\Gamma({\rm Ad}\,{\mathsf{P}}_{\rm G})\,
of global sections of the adjoint bundle (associated with the underlying principal
{\rm G}
-bundle
\,{\mathsf{P}}_{\rm G}
), isomorphic with the group
\,{\rm Hom}_{\rm G}({\mathsf{P}}_{\rm G},{\rm G})\,
of
{\rm G}
-equivariant maps from the total space of the principal
{\rm G}
-bundle to its structure group. The existence of a global section of
\,{\mathsf{P}}_{\rm G}\,
enables us to present gauge transformations as mappings from
\,[{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1},{\rm G}]
.

\,\chi\in[{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1},{\rm G}]\,
and written in terms of the embeddings
\,\iota_{i}\ :\ \ell_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}
.

In the convenient
static gauge

\,{\rm A}_{0}=0\,
of the connection 1-form

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t,\sigma)={\rm A}_{0}(t,\sigma)\,{\mathsf{d}}t+{\rm A}(t,\sigma)\,,\qquad\qquad{\rm A}(t,\sigma)={\rm A}_{a}(t,\sigma)\,{\mathsf{d}}\sigma^{a}

(expressed in local coordinates
\,(t,\sigma)\,
on
\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}
, with
\,\sigma\equiv(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})
), preserved by the static-gauge
transformations composing the effective gauge group
\,{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}=[{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1},{\rm G}]
, the field equations read

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rm A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,,

(4.35)

\displaystyle{\rm F}({\rm A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\,\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\gamma_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\,\delta_{P_{i}}-{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\gamma_{n+1}}(\lambda_{n+1})\,\delta_{P_{n+1}}\bigr)\,,

(4.37)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bigl({\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\gamma_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,,

where
\,\delta_{P_{i}}=\delta^{(2)}(\sigma-\sigma_{i})\,{\mathsf{d}}\sigma^{1}\wedge{\mathsf{d}}\sigma^{2}\,
(
\sigma_{i}\,
is the value taken by the local coordinates at
\,P_{i}
) and where
\,{\rm F}({\rm A})={\mathsf{d}}{\rm A}+{\rm A}\wedge{\rm A}\,
is the curvature of the connection (
i.e.
, the gauge-field strength). Thus, up to gauge transformations, the theory is effectively two-dimensional – there is no dynamics. In particular, it has a
finite-dimensional
(physical) phase space

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{0,n+1}=\Im_{0,n+1}/{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}

defined as the space of orbits of the effective gauge group
\,{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,
within the space
\,\Im_{0,n+1}\,
of flat connections on a (trivial) principal
{\rm G}
-bundle over the
decorated
Riemann sphere
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}
, also known as the
moduli space of flat connections
over
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}
. Just to reemphasise: The connection 1-forms are assumed smooth everywhere on
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}\,
except at the non-coincident points
\,P_{i},\ i\in\overline{1,n+1}
, where they are constrained to take values (up to gauge transformations) in the respective (co)adjoint orbits
26
26

26

Recall that we have identified
\,\mathfrak{g}^{*}\,
with
\,\mathfrak{g}\,
with the help of the Killing form.

\displaystyle\mathscr{O}_{\lambda_{i}}=\bigl\{\ \tfrac{2\pi{\mathsf{i}}}{{\mathsf{k}}}\,{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x}(\lambda_{i})\quad|\quad x\in{\rm G}\ \bigr\}\,.

The
unphysical
phase space
\,\Im_{0,n+1}\,
of the gauge theory comes with the presymplectic form

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm CS|W}[\mathcal{A},\gamma_{i}]=\Omega_{\mathscr{A}}[{\rm A}]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\varpi_{i}(\overline{\gamma}_{i})-\varpi_{n+1}(\overline{\gamma}_{n+1})\,,\qquad\overline{\gamma}_{i}=\gamma_{i}(0)\,,

easily derived (
e.g.
, in the first-order formalism of Refs.
[
Gaw72
,
Kij73
,
Kij74
,
KS76
,
Szc76
,
KT79
]
) in the form of the sum of the canonical Atiyah–Bott form

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathscr{A}}[{\rm A}]=\tfrac{{\mathsf{k}}}{4\pi}\,\int_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\delta{\rm A}\wedge\delta{\rm A})

of Ref.
[
AB82
]
and the Kirillov–Kostant–Souriau forms

\displaystyle\varpi_{i}={\mathsf{k}}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda_{i}\,{\mathsf{d}}\theta_{L})

on the respective
\,\mathscr{O}_{\lambda_{i}}
, the latter forms playing a central rôle in the geometric quantisation of coadjoint orbits, developed in Refs.
[
Kos70
,
Sou70
,
Kir75
]
.

In order to make contact with the structures appearing in the previous section, we should next extend
\,\Omega_{\rm CS|W}\,
(denoting it by
\,\Omega_{\mathscr{A}}^{\rm tot}\,
henceforth) to the space

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{0,n+1}^{\rm tot}=\mathscr{A}_{0,n+1}\times\times_{i=1}^{n+1}\,\mathscr{O}_{\lambda_{i}}

composed of the space
\,\mathscr{A}_{0,n+1}\,
of
all
connections on a (trivial) principal
{\rm G}
-bundle over the decorated Riemann sphere and of the coadjoint orbits
\,\mathscr{O}_{\lambda_{i}}\,
assigned to the punctures. It is not hard to demonstrate
27
27

27

Cf.
,
e.g.
, the arguments in Ref.
[
Sen02
]
.
that the pair
\,(\mathscr{A}_{0,n+1}^{\rm tot},\Omega_{\mathscr{A}}^{\rm tot})\,
is actually a
symplectic
space, and the gauge group
\,{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,
is readily seen to act on this space in a hamiltonian manner (in the sense of,
e.g.
, Ref.
[
Woo92
, Def. (3.2.3)]
), with the momentum

\displaystyle\mu\ :\ \mathscr{A}_{0,n+1}^{\rm tot}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}\ :\ \left({\rm A},{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{1}}(\lambda_{1}),{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{2}}(\lambda_{2}),\ldots,{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{n}}(\lambda_{n})\right)\longmapsto\mu_{\left({\rm A},{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\right)}

explicitly defined by the formula

\displaystyle\mu_{\left({\rm A},{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\right)}(X):=-\tfrac{{\mathsf{k}}}{2\pi}\,\int_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigl[X\,\bigl({\rm F}({\rm A})-2\pi\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{i}}(\lambda_{i})\,\delta_{P_{i}}+2\pi\,{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{x_{n+1}}(\lambda_{n+1})\,\delta_{P_{n+1}}\bigr)\bigr]\,,

valid for arbitrary
\,X\in\mathfrak{g}
. Hence, the unphysical phase space of the Chern–Simons theory on
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
described earlier coincides with the level set

\displaystyle\Im_{0,n+1}=\mu^{-1}\bigl(\{0\}\bigr)

of the moment map, and the
physical
phase space can be obtained through the symplectic reduction of the latter,

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{0,n+1}=\mu^{-1}\bigl(\{0\}\bigr)//{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,,

in the sense of Marsden and Weinstein,
cf.
Ref.
[
MW74
]
, with respect to the (co)isotropy group
\,{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\,
of the zero weight.

The significance of the last result rests upon the fact that it explicitly identifies the kernel of the presymplectic form

\,\Omega_{\rm CS|W}\,
and thus suggests symplectic reduction as the most natural procedure of establishing the symplectic structure on the finite-dimensional physical phase space of the Chern–Simons theory. In this manner, we arrive at the construction of Alekseev and Malkin which turns to account the non-trivial topology of the domain
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
of smoothness of the connection 1-forms so as to
partially
reduce the presymplectic space
\,(\Im_{0,n+1},\Omega_{\mathscr{A}}^{\rm tot})\,
with respect to a judiciously chosen infinite-dimensional subgroup of the gauge group, so that at the end of the day there emerges a finite-dimensional quotient equipped with a presymplectic form with a well-defined kernel spanned by generators of the residual gauge symmetry. The construction, outlined below, yields a 2-form related directly to the one appearing on the LHS of the DJI for the maximally symmetric WZW defect, given Eq. (
4.1
), together with a compact definition of its kernel.

We begin by picking up an arbitrary point
\,P_{*}\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
together with representatives
\,m_{i}\,
of the generators
\,[m_{i}]\,
of the based fundamental group
\,\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}};P_{*})
, subject to the single condition

\displaystyle[m_{1}\circ m_{2}\circ\cdots\circ m_{n}]=[m_{n+1}]

(here,
\,\circ\,
stands for the concatenation of loops), and subsequently cutting
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
along the
\,m_{i}\,
as in Figure
8
.

Figure 8.
Decomposition of the punctured Riemann sphere
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\vec{P}\}}\,
into a disjoint union of a bouquet of
n+1
punctured discs
\,{\mathbb{D}}_{i}\,
and an un-punctured one,
\,{\mathbb{D}}_{*}
, sewn (trivially) along the common boundary. The decomposition is achieved through incision of
\,{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\vec{P}\}}\,
along representatives
\,m_{i}\,
of the generators of
\,\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}{\rm P}^{1}_{\{\vec{P}\}};P_{*})
. The arrows piercing the
sphere represent the vertical timelike (resp. anti-timelike) Wilson lines
\,{\rm W}_{\lambda_{i\neq n+1}}\,
(resp.
\,{\rm W}_{\lambda_{n+1}}
) of the gauge field theory.

The homological surgery enables us to locally present the connection as a gauge transform of the trivial one,

\displaystyle{\rm A}|_{{\mathbb{D}}_{*}}=-\chi_{*}^{*}\theta_{\rm R}\,,\qquad\chi_{*}\in{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{*}}\,,

over the un-punctured disc, and as a gauge transform of a representative
\,\omega_{i}\,
of the generator class in
\,H^{1}({\mathbb{D}}_{i}\setminus\{P_{i}\},{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle{\rm A}|_{{\mathbb{D}}_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\delta_{i,n+1}}\,\tfrac{2\pi{\mathsf{i}}}{{\mathsf{k}}}\,\omega_{i}\otimes{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{\chi_{i}}(\lambda_{i})-\chi_{i}^{*}\theta_{\rm R}\,,\qquad\chi_{i}\in{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{D}}_{i}}\,,

over the punctured disc
\,{\mathbb{D}}_{i}
. The normalisation of
\,\omega_{i}\,
is such that
\,\int_{C_{i}}\,\omega_{i}=1\,
for an arbitrary contour
\,C_{i}\subset{\mathbb{D}}_{i}\,
that goes around
\,P_{i}\,
once. We can now substitute the local presentations of
\,{\rm A}\,
into the formula for
\,\Omega_{\rm CS|W}\,
(having split the two-dimensional integral into a sum of integrals over the
n+2
discs beforehand), whereby the latter is readily seen to reduce to a finite sum of contour integrals along the loops
\,m_{i}\,
and simple contributions from the punctures
\,P_{i}
. At this point, it remains to solve the smoothness condition for
\,{\rm A}
, imposed at the common boundary of
\,{\mathbb{D}}_{*}\,
and
\,{\mathbb{D}}_{1}\vee{\mathbb{D}}_{2}\vee\cdots\vee{\mathbb{D}}_{n+1}\,
and relating the boundary values of the restricted gauge transformations
\,\chi_{*}\,
and the
\,\chi_{i}
. The thus simplified pre-symplectic form can be expressed in terms of the
transport
(or
monodromy
) operators

\displaystyle M_{i}[\chi_{i}]=P{\rm e}^{\int_{m_{i}}\,{\rm A}}={\rm Ad}_{\chi_{i}(P_{*})}({\rm e}_{\lambda_{i}})\,,

defined with the help of the standard path-ordering operator
\,P\,
and subject to an obvious constraint

\displaystyle M_{1}[\chi_{1}]\cdot M_{2}[\chi_{2}]\cdot\cdots\cdot M_{n}[\chi_{n}]=M_{n+1}[\chi_{n+1}]\,.

These are manifestly invariant under gauge transformations
\,\chi\,
from the (infinite-dimensional) subgroup

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}(P_{*})=\{\ g\in{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}\quad|\quad g(P_{*})=e\ \}\subset{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}

of the gauge group,
\,M_{i}[\chi\cdot\chi_{i}]={\rm Ad}_{\chi(P_{*})}\left(M_{i}[\chi_{i}]\right)\equiv M_{i}[\chi_{i}]
, and so they coordinatise the
finite-dimensional
partial quotient

(4.38)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}:=\Im_{0,n+1}/{\rm G}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}}(P_{*})\equiv\{\ (M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{n},M)\in\times_{i=1}^{n}\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{i}}\times\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{n+1}}\quad|\quad M_{1}\cdot M_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot M_{n}=M\ \}\,,

(4.39)

embedded in the product manifold

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}:=\times_{i=1}^{n}\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{i}}\times\mathscr{C}_{\lambda_{n+1}}\,.

In conformity with the general scheme of symplectic reduction,
\,\Omega_{\rm CS|W}\,
descends to the quotient
\,\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}\,
as a presymplectic form
\,\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
with a finite-dimensional kernel spanned by the fundamental vector fields engendered by constant gauge transformations
\,M_{i}\longmapsto{\rm Ad}_{\chi}(M_{i}),\ \chi\in{\rm G}\,
from the residual gauge group
\,{\rm G}
. Parenthetically, let us also point out that the
{\rm G}
-invariance of the descended presymplectic form ensures the independence of the 2-form itself of the choice of the base point
\,P_{*}
. Indeed, changing the latter is tantamount to conjugating all transport operators by the same group element.

In order to be able to write out the descended presymplectic form, we parameterise
\,\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}\,
(in a manifestly redundant manner) with the help of the surjective map

(4.41)

\displaystyle\pi\

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\ {\rm G}^{\times n+1}\times T^{\times n+1}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\ (u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n},u,C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{n},C)\longmapsto\left({\rm Ad}_{u_{1}}(C_{1}),{\rm Ad}_{u_{2}}(C_{2}),\ldots,{\rm Ad}_{u_{n}}(C_{n}),{\rm Ad}_{u}(C)\right)\,,

written for the
(n+1)
-th cartesian power
\,T^{\times n+1}\,
of the Cartan subgroup
\,T\subset{\rm G}\,
of
\,{\rm G}
, whereupon we arrive at the definition

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0,n+1}:=\pi^{-1}(\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1})\,.

In this parametrisation, the 2-form
\,\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
of interest reads

(4.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\pi^{*}\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n},u,C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{n},C)=\tfrac{{\mathsf{k}}}{4\pi}\,{\rm tr}_{\mathfrak{g}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\theta_{L}(u_{i})\wedge{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{C_{i}}\circ\theta_{L}(u_{i})

(4.43)

(4.44)

\displaystyle-\theta_{L}(u)\wedge{\mathsf{T}}_{e}{\rm Ad}_{C}\circ\theta_{L}(u)+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\,\bigl(K_{j}^{*}\theta_{L}\wedge K_{j+1}^{*}\theta_{L}\bigr)(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n},C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{n})\bigg)\,,

where

\displaystyle K_{j}(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n},C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{n})={\rm Ad}_{u_{n+1-j}}(C_{n+1-j})\cdot{\rm Ad}_{u_{n+2-j}}(C_{n+2-j})\cdot\cdots\cdot{\rm Ad}_{u_{n}}(C_{n})\,.

As a corollary to Theorem 1 of Ref.
[
AM95
]
(explicitly stated in Ref.
[
AM95
, Eq. (3.8)]
), we obtain

Proposition 35
.

The kernel of
\,\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
is spanned by the fundamental vector fields for the left
{\rm G}
-action

(4.46)

\displaystyle{\rm G}\times\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\ \left(y,(M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{n},M)\right)\longmapsto\left({\rm Ad}_{y}(M_{1}),{\rm Ad}_{y}(M_{2}),\ldots,{\rm Ad}_{y}(M_{n}),{\rm Ad}_{y}(M)\right)\,.

By now, we have all the requisite tools for the solution of the original problem, which is the verification of the postulated (preliminary) localisation of the worldvolume of the WZW inter-bi-brane. To this end, we consider, once more, the manifold (
4.39
) and note that the latter is canonically diffeomorphic to the previously encountered manifold
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
of Eq. (
3.27
) as
per

(4.47)

\displaystyle\hbar\ :\ T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}\ :\ (h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n})\longmapsto(h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n},h_{1}\cdot h_{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot h_{n})\,.

The isomorphism gives us the fundamental

Proposition 36
.

Let
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
and
\,\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
be the 2-forms defined in Eqs. (
4.31
) and (
4.42
), respectively, and let
\,\hbar\,
be the diffeomorphism of Eq. (
4.47
). Then,

(4.48)

\displaystyle\hbar^{*}\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}=-\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,.

Proof.

Cf.
Appendix
F
. ∎

The above implies, in particular, that the kernel of
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
can be obtained through pushforward of
\,{\rm ker}\,\Omega_{\mathscr{F}_{0,n+1}}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
along the inverse of
\,\hbar
. This yields the all-important

Theorem 37

(The kernel of
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
)
.

Let
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
be the manifold defined in Eq. (
4.42
), and let the
\,L_{A}\,
(resp. the
\,R_{A}
) be the standard left-invariant (resp. right-invariant) vector fields on
\,{\rm G}
, cf. Eq. (
C.3
). The kernel of the 2-form
\,\Omega_{n}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\in\Omega^{2}(T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda})\,
of Eq. (
4.31
) is spanned by the fundamental vector fields

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}_{A}(h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,V_{A}(h_{i})\,,\qquad\qquad V_{A}(h_{i})=(R_{A}-L_{A})(h_{i})

of the left action of
\,{\rm G}\,
on
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
given by

\displaystyle{\rm G}\times T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\longrightarrow T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\ :\ \left(y,(h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n})\right)\longmapsto\left({\rm Ad}_{y}(h_{1}),{\rm Ad}_{y}(h_{2}),\ldots,{\rm Ad}_{y}(h_{n})\right)\,.

Proof.

The fundamental vector fields for the left
{\rm G}
-action on
\,\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}\,
push forward along
\,\hbar^{-1}\,
to the
\,\mathscr{V}_{A}
. The thesis now follows directly from that Prop.
35
.
∎

The theorem (partially) settles the issue of localisation of the elementary inter-bi-brane worldvolumes in that it proves that the
\,\mathcal{T}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}
, formerly shown to be the smallest
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-spaces that can support the inter-bi-brane data (
i.e.
, the fusion 2-isomorphism
\,\varphi_{n+1}
), are in fact also the largest spaces with this property. This, then, confirms the postulated decomposition of the (total) worldvolume of the inter-bi-brane (associated with defect junctions of a given valence) stated in Eq. (
4.29
). Further specification of the
worldvolume calls for heavier cohomological tools, such as,
e.g.
, an explicit construction of
\,\varphi_{n+1}\,
alluded to previously, an idea that we take up in the next section.

The foregoing analysis, including an exposition of the main conceptual points of the Alekseev–Malkin construction (itself embedded in the theory of quasi-hamiltonian
{\rm G}
-spaces,
cf.
Ref.
[
AMM98
]
), leaves us with the intriguing question as to a deeper reason for the existence of a relation between, on the one hand, the geometry of the phase space of the topological field theory defined by Eq. (
4.33
) and its symplectic reduction, and, on the other hand, by the gerbe-theoretic structure associated with junctions of the maximally symmetric WZW defect and represented implicitly by the DJI. While a full-fledged explanation of this relation falls well beyond the compass of the present paper (and would, among other things, prerequire an in-depth case study along the lines of Ref.
[
RS11
]
), we find it fitting to conclude this section with a heuristic argument that sets up the conceptual framework for further study of the relation.

The upshot of our hitherto analysis is a correspondence between supports of the fusion 2-isomorphisms
\,\varphi_{n+1}\,
(and hence also the corresponding component inter-bi-brane 2-isomorphisms and junctions of the maximally symmetric WZW defects) and (some of) leaves of the characteristic foliation of the partially symplectically reduced presymplectic form of the Chern–Simons theory with the Wilson lines
\,{\rm W}_{\lambda_{i}},\ i\in\overline{1,n+1}\,
on the cylinder
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
over the punctured Riemann sphere. The space of these leaves, denoted as
\,\mathscr{M}_{0,n+1}=\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}/{\rm G}
, constitutes the finite-dimensional (classical) space of states of the theory. Condition (
4.35
) for the connection which enters the definition of
\,\mathscr{H}_{0,n+1}\,
carries over to the (holomorphically) quantised theory as an operator constraint imposed on
physical
quantum states, admitting an interpretation of the (infinitesimal) Ward identity satisfied by the corresponding wave function(al)s. As discussed in,
e.g.
, Ref.
[
Gaw99
, Sec. 5]
, this permits to identify states of the Chern–Simons theory on
\,{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}{\mathsf{P}}^{1}_{\{\overrightarrow{P}\}}\,
as certain distinguished
intertwiners
of the (diagonal)
{\rm G}
-action on irreducible modules labelled by the weights
\,\lambda_{i}
. It deserves to be noted that it is precisely the Ward identity that enables to relate sesquilinear combinations of physical wave functions of the Chern–Simons theory to the so-called conformal blocks that compose correlation functions of the WZW model, thus establishing a profound correspondence between the two theories.

The connection with maximally symmetric WZW defect junctions suggested by the above collection of facts rests upon the following two related observations: First of all, as shown in Ref.
[
FFRS07
]
, a junction of maximally symmetric WZW defect lines has a well-defined description in the quantised WZW model iff the Verlinde fusion coefficient
\,\mathcal{N}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}}^{\hskip 35.0pt\lambda_{n+1}}\,
for the weights
\,\lambda_{i}\,
that label the defect lines converging at the junction is non-zero. However, the said coefficient is known (
cf.
Ref.
[
Wit89b
]
) to give the dimension of the space of conformal blocks on the Riemann sphere with insertions (at non-coincident points
\,P_{i},\ i\in\{1,n+1\}
) of primary operators of the WZW model from the respective integrable highest-weight modules labelled by the
\,\lambda_{i}
. Thus, the quantum junctions are rather straightforwardly linked to the quantum states of the Chern–Simons theory, and our findings reported hereinabove indicate that it is reasonable to expect that this link remains visible in the classical régime in the higher-geometric formulation. The second observation derives from the study, reported in Refs.
[
Sus11a
,
Sus12
]
, of the rôle played by the geometric data associated with conformal defects and their junctions in the canonical description of the
\sigma
-model, both classical and geometrically pre-quantised. In this description, to a given defect junction of valence
n+1
one assigns a subspace in the
(n+1)
-th power of the phase space of the
\sigma
-model by thinking of it as a vertex of a cross-defect splitting-joining interaction of the string (resp. of a splitting-joining interaction in the defect-twisted sector). As argued in the latter of the two articles, in the case of defects that preserve, in the sense of a continuity relation for the Noether currents, (a subset of) symmetries of the bulk
\sigma
-model on either side of the defect line, the field-space data for a junction define an intertwiner for the action of the preserved symmetry (sub)group on the factors in the product phase space assigned to the junction that correspond to the incoming and outgoing states, respectively. In the context of the WZW model, with its
maximally
symmetric defects, this brings us back to the description of states of the Chern–Simons theory in terms of group-
{\rm G}
intertwiners.

5.
The higher geometry of the WZW background

The in-depth geometric analysis of the previous section, crowned with the argument derived from the study of the topological Chern–Simons theory, has provided us with a simplicial
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-manifold

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 364.76347pt\raise-59.13937pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.68947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 8.19447pt\underline{d}{}^{(1)}_{1}\equiv\iota_{1}={\rm pr}_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 446.53676pt\raise-66.82884pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 367.201pt\raise-78.82384pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.68947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip as a tentative model of the target space of the maximally symmetric WZW defect, implemeting the binary operation of the underlying Lie group
\,{\rm G}
. In this model, there are three as yet unspecified objects: subsets
\,P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})\subset{\mathsf{k}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g})\,
and
\,\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1}}\backslash\times_{i=2}^{n}\,({\rm G}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{i}})
, and the submanifolds
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\subset T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}
. These are determined by certain higher-geometric structures over
\,\underline{{\mathsf{N}}}{}_{\bullet}{\rm G}\,
that realise the simplicial de Rham class
\,[({\rm H}_{\rm C}^{({\mathsf{k}})},\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})},0,0)]\,
and, in so doing, complete the definition of the maximally symmetric WZW background. Below, we specify these structures and discuss their properties with respect to the simplicial structure the
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-action on
\,\underline{{\mathsf{N}}}{}_{\bullet}{\rm G}
, thereby identifying the said objects.

Remark 38
.

Following the reasoning from Sec.
3.2
, one is tempted to draw yet another pencil of arrows pointing from
\,{\rm G}\times\bigsqcup_{(\overrightarrow{\lambda},\lambda)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})^{\times n+1}}\,\bigsqcup_{[\overrightarrow{w}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}}\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
to
\,{\rm G}\times\bigsqcup_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})^{\times 3}}\,\bigsqcup_{[w]\in\mathcal{F}_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\lambda}}\,T_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{[w]}\,
and representing (restrictions of) the mappings
\,{\rm id}_{\rm G}\times\bigsqcup_{(\overrightarrow{\lambda},\lambda)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})^{\times n+1}}\,\bigsqcup_{[\overrightarrow{w}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}}\,\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|k,l)\,
for arbitrary
\,1\leq i\leq j<k\leq l\leq n
. This, however, would presuppose the existence of certain relations between the
sub
sets
\,\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1}}\backslash\times_{i=2}^{n}\,({\rm G}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{i}})\,
and
\,\mathcal{F}_{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}^{\mu}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{1}}\backslash{\rm G}/\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{2}}\,
that we have not spelled out in Sec.
4.4
. In fact, such relations appear quite natural from the point of view of the two-dimensional conformal field theory under consideration, and so we come back to them when discussing the fusion 2-isomorphism(s) in Sec.
5.4
,

5.1.
The WZW 1-gerbe

We begin with the geometrisation of the bulk de Rham class
\,[{\rm H}_{\rm C}^{({\mathsf{k}})}]
,
i.e.
, a 1-gerbe over
\,{\rm G}\,
with curvature given by the
{\mathsf{k}}
-th multiple of the Cartan 3-form of Eq. (
4.3
). The first thing to be noted is that in order for the 1-gerbe to exist we need
\,[{\rm H}_{\rm C}^{({\mathsf{k}})}]\in H^{3}({\rm G},2\pi{\mathbb{Z}})\subset H^{3}({\rm G},{\mathbb{R}})
, which happens iff
\,{\mathsf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Taking into account the related normalisation of the metric term in the action functional of the WZW
\sigma
-model, we further restrict
\,{\mathsf{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so that the term has the standard sign. That the choice of the curvature of the 1-gerbe then determines the class of
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
uniquely follows from Prop.
16
and the isomorphism
\,H^{2}({\rm G},{\rm U}(1))\cong{\boldsymbol{1}}\,
(here, and in what follows,
\,{\boldsymbol{1}}\,
is the trivial group). In fact,
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
is 1-isomorphic with the
{\mathsf{k}}
-th tensor power
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\cong\mathcal{G}^{(1)\,\otimes{\mathsf{k}}}\,
of the so-called
basic gerbe
28
28

28

Meinrenken’s construction of the basic gerbe for a general 1-connected Lie group was preceded by that of Ref.
[
Gaw88
]
for
\,{\rm SU}(2)\,
and that of Ref.
[
Cha98
]
for
\,{\rm SU}(N)
. The non-simply connected case was worked out in Refs.
[
GR02
,
GR03
]
.

\,\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\,
of Ref.
[
Mei03
]
, of curvature
\,{\rm curv}(\mathcal{G}^{(1)})={\rm H}_{\rm C}
. The bi-invariance of the Cartan 3-form then implies, by the very same token, that the 1-gerbe is
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-symmetric,

\displaystyle{\rm curv}\bigl(\ell\wp_{(x,y)}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\bigr)=\ell\wp_{(x,y)}^{*}{\rm curv}\bigl(\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\bigr)={\rm curv}\bigl(\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\bigr)\qquad{\mathrel{\to}^{\ \ H^{2}({\rm G},{\rm U}(1))\cong{\boldsymbol{1}}\ \ }}\qquad\ell\wp_{(x,y)}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\cong\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,.

We may next enquire, with hindsight, about the existence of a (generalised) multiplicative structure on
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
, a natural higher-cohomological analogon of the concepts of a group homomorphism and that of a multiplicative line bundle over a group manifold, introduced in Ref.
[
CJM+05
]
and further elaborated in Refs.
[
Wal10
,
GW09
]
, which we recalled and generalised in Def.
28
with view to subsequent considerations in the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded setting. In virtue of the Universal Coefficient Theorem, in conjunction with the Künneth Formula, we obtain, for
\,{\rm G}\,
simple and 1-connected,
\,H^{2}({\rm G}\times{\rm G},{\rm U}(1))={\boldsymbol{1}}=H^{1}({\rm G}\times{\rm G},{\rm U}(1))
, and so the above identity together with Prop.
16
immediately implies the existence of a 1-isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\ :\ \mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{2}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{12}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}}\,,\qquad\qquad\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}={\mathsf{k}}\,\varrho\,,

cf.
Eq. (
4.15
), which is unique up to a 2-isomorphism by Prop.
17
. Note that by the previous cohomological arguments and in consequence of the elementary property

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(2;2)}_{\rm G}\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}=0

of the 2-form
\,\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}
,
cf.
Eq. (
4.16
), a 2-isomorphism (
3.11
) with
\,\vartheta=0\,
always exists on a 1-connected group
\,{\rm G}
. As anticipated in Sec.
3.1
, the additional structure
\,(\mathcal{M},\alpha)\,
plays a central rôle in the construction of the maximally symmetric WZW bi-brane proposed in Ref.
[
FSW08
]
, linking the latter to the long-known construction of the maximally symmetric brane in a manner essentially determined by the guidelines on p.
3.1
and symmetry considerations. For the sake of a succinct expression of this fact in the subsequent sections, we fix below one further piece of notation.

The point of departure is the natural correspondence between, on the one hand, collections of binary operations on adjacent pairs of cartesian components of
\,{\rm G}^{\times n},\ n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}\,
(or
multiplication patterns
) and, on the other hand, full binary trees with
n
leaves of Def.
20
. For
\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}
, the correspondence can be neatly encoded in a map

\displaystyle\mu_{n}\ :\ {\rm FBT}_{n}\longrightarrow 2^{C^{\infty}({\rm G}^{\times n},{\rm G}^{\times 2})}

that assigns to a given tree
\,\tau\in{\rm FBT}_{n}\,
the collection of those smooth maps
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}(i,j|j+1,k)\,
of Eq. (
3.30
) that are represented by its non-unary vertices (or, equivalently, by pairs of its edges joined by one of its vertices),
e.g.
,

\displaystyle\mu_{3}(\tau_{3}^{\rm L})=\{{\rm m}^{(3)}(1,1|2,2),{\rm m}^{(3)}(1,2|3,3)\}\,,

cf.
(
1.78
). Our presentation of the correspondence is fixed by setting

\displaystyle\mu_{2}\ :\ {\rm FBT}_{2}\longrightarrow 2^{C^{\infty}({\rm G}^{\times 2},{\rm G}^{\times 2})}\ :\ \tau^{\rm L}_{2}\longmapsto{\rm id}_{{\rm G}^{\times 2}}\,.

In parallel, we may also define (for
\,n\geq 2
) a map

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{n}\ :\ {\rm FBT}_{n}\longrightarrow 1\textrm{-}{\rm Iso}\left(\mathfrak{BGrb}^{\nabla}({\rm G}^{\times n})\right)

that assigns to a given tree
\,\tau\in{\rm FBT}_{n}\,
a sequence of pullbacks, along elements of
\,\mu_{n}(\tau)
, of the 1-isomorphism
\,\mathcal{M}\,
of the multiplicative structure that realises the multiplication pattern represented by
\,\tau\,
on the relevant tensor product of pullbacks of the 1-gerbe
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
,
e.g.
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{3}(\tau_{3}^{\rm L})=\mathcal{M}_{12,3}\circ(\mathcal{M}_{1,2}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{G}_{3}})\equiv{\rm m}^{(3)}(1,2|3,3)^{*}\mathcal{M}\circ\bigl({\rm m}^{(3)}(1,1|2,2)^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{G}_{3}}\bigr)\,.

As a simple consequence of the associativity of the multiplicative structure on
\,\mathcal{G}
, we readily establish

Corollary 39
.

For every
\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 3}
, there exists a map

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{n}\ :\ {\rm FBT}_{n}\longrightarrow 2\textrm{-}{\rm Iso}\left(\mathfrak{BGrb}^{\nabla}({\rm G}^{\times n})\right)

such that, for a given tree
\,\tau\in{\rm FBT}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{n}(\tau)\ :\ \mathscr{M}_{n}(\tau_{n}^{\rm L}){\mathrel{\to}^{\ \cong\ }}\mathscr{M}_{n}(\tau)\,.

The thesis of the corollary will be seen to play an important rôle in the derivation of linear relations between various explicit realisations of the inter-bi-brane 2-isomorphism obtained through the so-called induction scheme of Ref.
[
RS09
, Sec. 2.8]
.

5.2.
The maximally symmetric WZW brane

Let us, next, consider the gerbe-theoretic data for circular (
i.e.
, non-intersecting) maximally symmetric WZW boundaries, that is the data of the maximally symmetric
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
-brane. While we are ultimately interested in WZW defects exclusively, the
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
-brane structure can be seen to form part of the gerbe-theoretic data of the
\mathcal{G}
-bi-brane, and so we spend some time recalling its details. As argued in Ref.
[
RS09
, p. 12]
(
cf.
also
4.2
), incorporating worldsheet boundaries into the unified framework of description valid for all defects, prerequires choosing the target-space in a rather special form, to wit, as the manifold
\,M={\rm G}\sqcup\{\bullet\}\,
with
\,\bullet\,
a point, and with – as previously – the 1-gerbe
\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
over
\,{\rm G}
. We fix this choice (solely) for the present section.

The presence of topological obstructions to the existence of a higher-geometric structure (the relevant gerbe module) over a generic conjugacy class
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}\,
leads to a quantisation of the weight label in an otherwise continuous set of orbits of the adjoint action within
\,{\rm G}
, confining the label to the fundamental affine Weyl alcove

\displaystyle P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})={\mathsf{k}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g})\cap P(\mathfrak{g})\ni\lambda\,,

given as the intersection of the weight lattice
\,P(\mathfrak{g})\,
of
\,\mathfrak{g}\,
with
\,{\mathsf{k}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\rm W}(\mathfrak{g})
,
cf.
Refs.
[
CJM04
,
GR02
,
Gaw05
]
.

The defect line
\,\ell\cong{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\,
is then mapped by the
\sigma
-model field
\,g\ :\ \ell\longrightarrow Q_{\partial}\,
to the disjoint union

(5.2)

\displaystyle Q_{\partial}=\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})}\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}

of orbits of the adjoint
{\rm G}
-action, the conjugacy classes of Cartan elements
\,{\mathsf{e}}_{\lambda}\,
of Eq. (
4.10
) labelled by weights from
\,P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})
. The conjugacy classes compose the worldvolume of the maximally symmetric
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
-brane

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\partial}=(Q_{\partial},\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\omega_{\partial},\Phi_{\partial})

with the following additional data: The curvature of the bi-brane is given by the 2-form
\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}\,
of Eq. (
4.14
), ensuring the existence of the
{\mathsf{L}}{\rm G}
-action on field configurations from
\,\mathcal{F}(\Sigma,d;\gamma)
subject to boundary conditions
\,X(\Sigma^{(1)})\subset Q_{\partial}
. The bi-brane maps are
\,\iota_{\lambda}\equiv\iota_{1}|_{\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}\ :\ \mathscr{C}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow{\rm G}\,
(the embedding of the conjugacy class
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}\,
in the group manifold), and the constant map
\,\iota_{2}=\bullet:Q_{\partial}-->\{\bullet\}
. Finally, the bi-brane 1-isomorphism is glued up from the
trivialisations

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\equiv\Phi_{\partial}|_{\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}\ :\ \iota_{\lambda}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\equiv\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}|_{\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial,\lambda}}\,.

It is vital to note that – as was shown for
\,{\rm G}={\rm SU}(N)\,
in Ref.
[
GR02
, Sec. 8.1]
, and for an arbitrary compact simple 1-connected Lie group
\,{\rm G}\,
in Ref.
[
Gaw05
, Sec. 5.1]
– the 1-isomorphisms
\,\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\,
exist for
\,\lambda\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})\,
exclusively, and so they single out the subset
\,Q_{\partial}\,
of conjugacy classes in
\,{\rm G}\,
which coincides with the set of worldvolumes of stable (untwisted) maximally symmetric D-branes of the WZW model at level
\,{\mathsf{k}}
,
cf.
,
e.g.
, Ref.
[
FFFS00
]
. These are, in turn, in a one-to-one correspondence with the (untwisted) maximally symmetric boundary states of the associated BCFT (
ib.
). It is also worth mentioning that the 1-isomorphisms are unique up to a (unique) 2-isomorphism due to the 1-connectedness of the
\,\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}
. They are also
{\rm Ad}
-symmetric, by the same token.

5.3.
The maximally symmetric WZW bi-brane

The species of bi-brane central to our considerations provides the data of the non-boundary maximally symmetric WZW defects, implementing jumps by elements of the target Lie group in the sense expressed by formulæ (
4.19
) and (
4.24
). A distinguished class of such defects – the central-jump defects at which
\,g_{|2}=g_{|1}\cdot z\,
for
\,z\,
from the centre
\,Z({\rm G})\,
of
\,{\rm G}\,
– alongside the attendant bi-brane were considered at length in Ref.
[
RS09
]
. (One should also mention, parenthetically, the gauge-symmetry defect of Ref.
[
Sus12
]
(
cf.
also Ref.
[
Sus13
]
) which in the current setting would realise a worldsheet-
local
variant of the action
\,\ell\wp\,
under restriction to a gauge-able subgroup
\,{\rm G}_{\rm gauge}\subset{\rm G}\times{\rm G}\,
of the bulk rigid-symmetry group (
e.g.
, the diagonal subgroup
\,\Delta({\rm G})
.) The more general jump defects, with the jump from a fixed conjugacy class in
\,{\rm G}
, were first discussed in Ref.
[
FSW08
]
, where the notion of a bi-brane was introduced. As emphasised in Sec.
4.3
, the
\sigma
-model field maps the defect line into the collection
\,Q={\rm G}\times Q_{\partial}\,
of full
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-orbits, given by the cartesian product of the group manifold with the worldvolume of the maximally symmetric
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
-brane. The ensuing maximally symmetric
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
-bi-brane

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\bigl(Q,\underline{d}{}^{(1)}_{1},\underline{d}{}^{(1)}_{0},\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})},\Phi\bigr)

has
\,Q\subset\underline{{\mathsf{N}}}{}_{1}{\rm G}\,
as its worldvolume, the 2-form
\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}\,
from Eq. (
4.25
) (fixed by the symmetry argument) as its curvature, the restrictions (to
\,Q
) of the lowest-level face maps of the simplicial
{\rm G}\times{\rm G}
-space
\,\underline{{\mathsf{N}}}{}_{\bullet}{\rm G}\,
(
i.e.
, of the source and target maps of
\,{\rm G}\rtimes{\rm G}
) as the bi-brane maps,

\displaystyle\iota_{1}=\underline{d}{}^{(1)}_{1}\,,\qquad\qquad\iota_{2}=\underline{d}{}^{(1)}_{0}\,,

and the composite 1-isomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi=\bigl(\mathcal{M}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{I}_{-{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}}}\bigr)\circ\bigl({\rm id}_{\underline{d}{}^{(1)\,*}_{1}\mathcal{G}}\otimes{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\Phi_{\partial}^{-1}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{I}_{-{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}}}\bigr)

as the last piece of its data. Here, it is understood that
\,\Phi\,
acts on the gerbe
\,\underline{d}{}^{(1)\,*}_{1}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial}}\equiv\underline{d}{}^{(1)\,*}_{1}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}\equiv\iota_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}
, as required by the definition of the bi-brane.

It ought to be underlined that – once again – the component bi-brane bi-module

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}:=\Phi|_{{\rm G}\times\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}=\bigl(\mathcal{M}|_{{\rm G}\times\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{I}_{-{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial,\lambda}}}\bigr)\circ\bigl({\rm id}_{\underline{d}{}^{(1)\,*}_{1}\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}}\otimes{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\otimes{\rm id}_{\mathcal{I}_{-{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial,\lambda}}}\bigr)

over
\,{\rm G}\times\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}\,
is unique up to a (unique) 2-isomorphism for
\,{\rm G}\,
a compact simple 1-connected Lie group. This, in conjunction with the unobstructed existence (and uniqueness up to a unique 2-isomorphism) of the 1-isomorphism
\,\mathcal{M}\,
determines the worldvolume of the maximally symmetric WZW bi-brane
\,\mathcal{B}\,
in terms of its boundary counterpart.

5.4.
The fusion 2-isomorphism

In this last section of our elucidation of the gerbe-theoretic structure associated with the maximally symmetric WZW defect, we give the definition and perform a (re)construction of the inter-bi-brane for the corresponding defect junctions of arbitrary valence. These defect junctions are particularly important for the WZW model as the operators that represent them in the quantum theory are enumerated by the Verlinde fusion coefficients of the RCFT, and so establishing a one-to-one correspondence between these coefficients (or the vector spaces of which they are dimensions) and ‘inequivalent’ (in a suitable sense) 2-isomorphisms would be another piece of strong evidence in support of the claim that the gerbe theory of the
classical

\sigma
-model encodes a lot of significant information on the quantum theory. The first step towards this goal consists in writing out the very definition of the inter-bi-brane 2-isomorphism
29
29

29

In the diagrams and formulæ of the present section, we are dropping the obvious subscripts from some identity morphisms for the sake of brevity. These can be reproduced easily from the context.
, along the lines of Ref.
[
RS09
]
, which may subsequently be employed to fix the remaining data of the inter-bi-brane, as illustrated in the case study reported in Ref.
[
RS11
]
.

Putting together the results of Sec.
4.4
and the constraints on the admissible weight labels reviewed above, we obtain the inter-bi-brane worldvolume

\displaystyle T=\bigsqcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 2}}\,T_{n,1}\,,\qquad T_{n,1}={\rm G}\times\bigsqcup_{(\overrightarrow{\lambda},\lambda)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})^{\times n+1}}\,\bigsqcup_{[\overrightarrow{w}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}}\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,,

with the connected components defined in Eq. (
3.25
). The (component) inter-bi-brane then takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{n,1}=\bigl(T_{n,1},\pi^{(n+1)}_{1,2},\pi^{(n+1)}_{2,3},\ldots,,\pi^{(n+1)}_{n,n+1},\pi^{(n+1)}_{1,n+1},\varphi_{n+1}\bigr)\,,

with the inter-bi-brane maps as in Eq. (
), and with the 2-isomorphism over

\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\bullet}

in which

is the universal 2-form (
4.31
), obtained here with the help of identity (

A
have been employed. Repeated use of the 2-isomorphism
\,\alpha\,
from Diagram (
3.11

\,\vartheta=0
), in conjunction with the canonical ‘death’ 2-isomorphism

per

\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23\cdots n}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\sum_{j=2}^{n}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23\cdots j,j+1}-\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,2}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1234}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1,2}+\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{12,3}+\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{123,4}-\sum_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{234}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{2,3}+\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23,4}-\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,2}-\sum_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{123}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1,2}+\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{12,3}-\sum_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{2,3}-\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,2}-\sum_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{12}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=3}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1,2}-\sum_{k=3}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{1,23\cdots n+1}^{-1}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\ {\rm id}\ }
\scriptstyle{\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{123,4}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{23,4}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{12,3}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2,3}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{1,23\cdots n+1}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{1,234}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{1,23}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{1,2}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1,2,3}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1,23,4}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\cdots}
\scriptstyle{d_{\mathcal{M}_{1,23\cdots n+1}}}

This permits us to extract from the former diagram the subdiagram

(5.6)

\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{2,3}-\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,2}-\sum_{k=4}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{234}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{2,3}+\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23,4}-\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,2}-\sum_{k=5}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n+1}\,\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{-\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial\,k}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{23\cdots n+1}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\,\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{12\cdots j,j+1}-\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1,23\cdots n+1}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\mathcal{G}^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\Omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{n\,2,3,\ldots,n+1}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=2}^{n+1}\,\Phi_{\partial\,k}^{-1}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2,3}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{23,4}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\vdots}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}\otimes\Phi_{\partial\,{23\cdots n+1}}\otimes{\rm id}}
\scriptstyle{\ {\rm id}\ }
\scriptstyle{\ \widetilde{\varphi}{}_{n+1}\ }

with the composite 2-isomorphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}{}_{n+1}:=\varphi_{n+1}\bullet\delta_{n+1}^{-1}

that we are after. The latter has been expressed in terms of the 2-isomorphism

(5.8)

\displaystyle\delta_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm id}\circ\bigl[d_{\mathcal{M}_{1,23\cdots n+1}}\bullet\bigl({\rm id}\circ(\alpha_{1,23\cdots n,n+1}\otimes{\rm id})\bigr)\bullet\bigl({\rm id}\circ(\alpha_{1,23\cdots n-1,n}\otimes{\rm id})\bigr)

\displaystyle\bullet\cdots\bullet\bigl({\rm id}\circ(\alpha_{1,2,3}\otimes{\rm id})\bigr)\bigr]\circ{\rm id}\,.

In order to draw further conclusions from the above reasoning, we need the following straightforward

109.24817pt\raise-68.3562pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varrho^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{1,2}+{\rm m}^{*}\omega^{({\mathsf{k}})}_{\partial,\lambda}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}

shall be called
basic
.

The sets
\,\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}
, first mentioned in Sec.
4.4
, are characterised by the condition

\displaystyle[\overrightarrow{w}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \textrm{there exists a fusion 2-isomorphism }\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\in 2\textrm{-}{\rm Iso}\left(\mathfrak{BGrb}^{\nabla}(T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]})\right)\,.

Thus, in particular,
\,\mathcal{F}_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}=\emptyset\,
iff the manifold
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
does not support a fusion 2-isomorphism.

\diamond

Drawing motivation (in part) from the explicit construction carried
out for
\,{\rm G}={\rm SU}(2)\,
in Ref.
[
RS11
]
, we propose

Conjecture 1
.

Let
\,\mathcal{N}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\ldots\lambda_{n}}^{\hskip 30.0pt\lambda}\in{\mathbb{N}}\,
be
the structure constants of the Verlinde fusion ring giving the
multiplicity of the chiral sector
\,[\lambda]\,
of the WZW
\sigma
-model
in the fusion product of the chiral sectors
\,[\lambda_{i}]
,

\displaystyle[\lambda_{1}]\star[\lambda_{2}]\star\cdots\star[\lambda_{n}]=\sum_{\lambda\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})}\,\mathcal{N}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\ldots\lambda_{n}}^{\hskip 30.0pt\lambda}\,[\lambda]\,.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\ldots\lambda_{n}}^{\hskip 30.0pt\lambda}=|\mathcal{F}_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})}^{\lambda}|\,.

In other words, the direct summands of type
\,[\lambda]\,
in the
decomposition of the fusion product
\,[\lambda_{1}]\star[\lambda_{2}]\star\cdots\star[\lambda_{n}]\,
are in a one-to-one
correspondence with the disjoint components
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
in the decomposition of the manifold

\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
into connected
{\rm G}
-submanifolds.

\boxtimes

We shall next take a closer look at some intricate constructive
implications of Definition
41
. As follows from the
general discussion in Sec.
1.3
, any two fusion
2-isomorphisms on the connected manifold

\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
differ by a phase
factor (a locally constant
{\rm U}(1)
-valued map), and a redefinition

\displaystyle\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\longmapsto\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\cdot c_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,,\qquad c_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\in{\rm U}(1)

of a given fusion 2-isomorphism
\,\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,
by an arbitrary phase factor

\,c_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,
produces an
admissible fusion 2-isomorphism. This observation, in conjunction
with our earlier discussion of the maps

\,\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|k,l)\,

of Eq. (
3.28
), prompts us to enquire as to the
possibility of realising in the present setting the induction scheme
put forward in Ref.
[
RS09
, Sec. 2.8]
and further
elaborated in the context of
\sigma
-model symmetries in
Ref.
[
Sus11a
, Rem. 5.6]
and
Ref.
[
Sus12
, Sec. 8.3]
. The idea is to use the said maps
and certain distinguished basic fusion 2-isomorphisms
\,\varphi_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\lambda}[w]\,
to derive a fusion 2-isomorphism

\,\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,
through a
systematic construction that we proceed to describe. Let us begin
with

Proposition 42
.

A fusion 2-isomorphism
\,\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,
exists over
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
iff there exists a 2-isomorphism

for an arbitrary tree
\,\tau\in{\rm PBT}_{n}
, and the two are
related (up to a phase factor) through the equality

\displaystyle\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]_{\tau}=c\cdot({\rm id}_{{\rm m}_{12\ldots n}^{*}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\otimes{\rm id}}\circ\mathscr{A}_{n}(\tau))\bullet\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}[\overrightarrow{w}]\,,

written for some constant
\,c\in{\rm U}(1)
.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of Cor.
39
. ∎

A priori, the proposition affords the possibility of
building a fusion 2-isomorphism over a given manifold

\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
in
\,|{\rm FBT}_{n}|\,
ways. Indeed, for each (planar) binary tree

\,\tau\,
representing a specific multiplication scheme, we may,
apparently
, write the corresponding fusion 2-isomorphism as a composition of
pullbacks along the respective maps
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}_{(i,j|j+1,k)}\in\mu(\tau)\,
of basic fusion 2-isomorphisms associated with the
binary vertices of
\,\tau
. An example of this construction (for

\,\tau=\tau_{n}^{\rm L}
) is presented in the diagram below,

The above reconstruction procedure may, however, encounter a topological
obstruction: Whenever at least one of the maps

\,[W]\circ\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|j+1,k)\,
for
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}(i,j|j+1,k)\in\mu_{n}(\tau)\,
sends
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
outside the corresponding nonempty
\,\mathcal{F}_{(\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j),\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(j+1,k))}^{\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,k)}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j)}\backslash{\rm G}/\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(j+1,k)}\,
with
\,\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,k)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})
, there is no basic fusion 2-isomorphism to pull
back to the vertex. A natural point of departure for the analysis of
the obstruction is an explicit verification of Conjecture

1
which is available only in the
particular case of
\,{\rm G}={\rm SU}(2)\,
as of this writing. The expected
correspondence of the gerbe structures under consideration with data
of the RCFT of the WZW
\sigma
-model leads us to postulate further

Conjecture 2
.

For all
\,\tau\in{\rm FBT}_{n}\,
and each
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}(i,j|j+1,k)\in\mu_{n}(\tau)
, the manifold
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\,
is mapped by
\,[W]\circ\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|j+1,k)\,
to the set
\,\mathcal{F}_{(\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j),\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(j+1,k))}^{\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,k)}\neq\emptyset
, so that the maximally symmetric WZW background is descent-complete in the sense of Def.
23
.

\boxtimes

Whenever the obstruction is absent, we have

Proposition 43
.

Let
\,\tau\in{\rm FBT}_{n}\,
be such that for each
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}(i,j|j+1,k)\in\mu_{n}(\tau)\,
the manifold
\,T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}\subset T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{\lambda}\,
is mapped by
\,[W]\circ\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|j+1,k)\,
to the set

\,\mathcal{F}_{(\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j),\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(k,l))}^{\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,k)}\neq\emptyset\,
with
\,\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,k)\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then, there exists a fusion 2-isomorphism

\,\varphi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}[\tau]\,
given
by a vertical composition of pullbacks of
\,n\,
basic fusion
2-isomorphisms from the manifolds
\,T^{[W]_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|j+1,k)}_{(\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j),\Lambda_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(j+1,k))}\equiv\pi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]}(i,j|j+1,k)(T_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}^{[\overrightarrow{w}]})\,
along (all)
the respective maps
\,{\rm m}^{(n)}(i,j|j+1,k)\,
as described in Def.
23
.

Proof.

Obvious.

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phi_{[w_{123}^{(2)}]\lambda_{23}[w_{23}],[w_{12}]\lambda_{12}[w_{123}^{(1)}]}^{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\ \lambda}[w_{2},w_{3}]\cdot c_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}^{\lambda_{23}}[w_{23}]^{-1}\cdot c_{(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{3})}^{\lambda}[w_{123}^{(1)}]\cdot c_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{23})}^{\lambda}[w_{123}^{(2)}]^{-1}\cdot c_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\lambda_{12}}[w_{12}]

that correspond to phase rescalings of the basic fusion
2-isomorphisms.

Our considerations lead us naturally to

Definition 45
.

Let
\,\phi:=\{\varphi_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{[w]}\}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda\in P^{{\mathsf{k}}}_{+}(\mathfrak{g}),[w]\in\mathcal{F}_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\lambda}}\,
be a collection of
basic fusion 2-isomorphisms, defined over the respective connected
submanifolds
\,T_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{[w]}\subset T_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}^{\lambda}
. The
maximally
symmetric WZW inter-bi-brane fusing matrix
for
